FAA grounds departing flights nationwide, citing air traffic communication system outage

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Federal Aviation Administration halted thousands of departing flights nationwide on Friday afternoon, a rare measure, blaming communications issues that affected "multiple" air traffic control centers. More than 10,000 flights were disrupted.

Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said Friday at the White House that there was no indication that the issues were a threat to the United States.

The FAA said in post on X that there was a "temporary telecommunications outage" and that it halted flights out of an "abundance of caution." The issues were related to communications and radar problems, according to an internal message from Alaska Airlines, which was seen by CNBC.

Airlines said en route flights would continue, but carriers were preparing to cancel flights as the disruption wore on. JetBlue Airways told staff it would cancel the remainder of the day's flights, aside from European routes, as well as others scheduled for Saturday so that crews and planes are in place to resume operations, according to a memo seen by CNBC.

FAA administrator Bryan Bedford had a call with airline CEOs around 6 p.m. indicating that it was unclear when the situation would be resolved, according to people familiar with the matter.

As of 5:56 p.m. ET, some outbound international traffic was in the air, according to Flightradar24, flight-tracking site. That included a Lufthansa flight from Boston to Frankfurt, Germany, that departed at 5:21 p.m. ET and an ITA Airways flight from Boston to Rome that left at 5:24 p.m. ET, according to the site.

As of 6:50 p.m. ET, cargo and passenger flights were also departing the West Coast for Asia and Hawaii.

Flights will be allowed to depart as en route traffic facilities recover, "through coordination with the air traffic control system," the FAA said at about 6 p.m..

Airlines said flights in the air would continue.

When it issued the ground stop at about 4 p.m., the FAA said the communications issues were "impacting multiple Air Route Traffic Control Centers." These facilities guide airplanes at high altitudes along their routes.

The FAA's technicians are investigating, the agency said.

A ground stop halts flights at their departure point. Disruptions tend to snowball if ground stops drag on as flight crews reach their maximum number of duty hours for a day.

There were 11,000 flights scheduled to depart U.S. airports after 4 p.m. ET on Friday, according to flight-data firm Cirium. There were about 600 diverted flights on Friday, 104 of them sent to other airports, Cirium said.

Nationwide ground stops are extremely rare.

In January 2023, the FAA halted flights because of issues with a pilot alert system. Before that, traffic was halted nationwide after the Sept. 11, 2001, terror attacks.

Technical issues have repeatedly snarled air traffic in the U.S. in recent years.

In September, hundreds of flights into Newark, New Jersey, and Philadelphia were canceled, delayed or diverted after a Verizon fiber cable was accidentally cut. Officials said it occurred during construction in New Jersey, severing a backup feed of key data for air traffic controllers.

In 2025, air traffic controllers lost contact with aircraft heading to and from Newark Liberty International Airport, an equipment failure that led to massive flight delays and raised more concerns about aging U.S. aviation infrastructure and staffing shortages.

It wasn't immediately clear what caused Friday's problem.

Inbound international flights will be managed on a case by case basis, the FAA said.

The major U.S. airlines issued statements saying they would work with customers who were affected by the ground stop and encouraged travelers to check for updates.

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger and Phil LeBeau contributed to this article.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/ardent-health-acquiring-missouri-nonprofit-boone-health-47m" hreflang="en">Ardent Health acquiring Missouri nonprofit Boone Health for $47M</a>

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Ardent Health acquiring Missouri nonprofit Boone Health for $47M" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Ardent Health is acquiring Boone Health, a mid-sized nonprofit system based in Columbia, Missouri, for $47 million in cash, the organizations announced Thursday. It marks Ardent's first entry into the Missouri healthcare market.

The deal, expected to close in the first quarter of 2027, includes a 10-year, $200 million capital commitment on nonprofit Ardent’s part. That money will be invested in Boone’s facilities, workforce and, in the near term, an expansion of its emergency department, according to the announcement.

Ardent has agreed to maintain Boone’s essential hospital services while developing its 392-bed regional referral center, Boone Hospital Center. It also plans to maintain employment for existing Boone employees and providers, continue its charity care policy and establish a local advisory board.

“We are acquiring a strong asset in an attractive and growing midsize market with significant opportunity to drive operating and financial improvement, all while delivering high patient quality care and safety,” Ardent Health President and CEO Dave Caspers said during a Thursday afternoon investor call discussing the deal. “It also represents an effective use of capital at a compelling valuation that we expect will create long-term shareholder value.”

Alongside its main hospital, Ardent would acquire Boone’s medical group, 19 care sites including an outpatient campus and five medical office buildings, related ancillary services and real estate. The system is the second-largest provider in its region with about 30% market share and half a billion dollars of annual revenue, Caspers said, with a local population that is well insured (93% coverage) and growing well ahead of the national average (3.4% in the next five years).

“Boone expands our footprint into a new attractive market,” Caspers explained. “It diversifies our business and enhances scale. Further, adding Boone's regional system to our portfolio is consistent with our strategy of building leading healthcare platforms in growing mid-sized communities. We're highly aligned with Boone Health on culture and mission, including putting the patient at the center of everything we do, with an aim toward best-in-class clinical care, patient quality, safety and access to services.”

The executive noted to investors that Boone’s facilities are relatively modern and well maintained, allowing Ardent to direct more capital toward its growth. The company has also identified “meaningful opportunities to optimize financial performance.” By leveraging its larger scale and expertise to tackle costs (supply chain procurement, vendor consolidation) and revenue cycle processes, Ardent executives said they expect to generate about $5 million to $10 million additional EBITDA within the deal’s first year, and at least $30 million of annual synergies three years after the deal’s close.

Boone Health has been independent since 2021, following over three decades during which it operated as part of the BJC HealthCare system. In 2025, its leadership kicked off a search for potential strategic partners “that could provide additional capital and resources to support the system's continued growth. Ardent was selected after a comprehensive evaluation process led by an independent advisory firm,” according to Thursday’s announcement.

“In Ardent, we identified a partner that supports our mission, respects our culture and can accelerate our vision without compromising what makes Boone Health special,” Jerry Kennett, M.D., chair of Boone’s Board of Trustees, said in the announcement. "Following a thoughtful review, the Board is confident Ardent is the right long-term partner for Boone Health. This agreement protects what our community has built while giving Boone Health the resources it needs to grow."

Ardent Health’s current operations include 30 acute care hospitals and over 280 care sites, several of which are run as part of a joint venture structure with other health system partners. In 2025, the company increased its revenues by 6% to $6.3 billion and grew its adjusted EBITDA to $545 million.

The company recently logged a strong third quarter off the back of solid demand for emergent care and progress on its ongoing performance improvement initiative. In September, it also officially divested from a joint venture with the University of Kansas Health System, a decision that leadership said is part of an ongoing review of its portfolio to ensure long-term strategic fit among its hospitals and partnerships.

Serie A, 320 milioni da Avenue Capital ma alcuni club frenano: ecco perché l'accordo è a rischio

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Serie A, 320 milioni da Avenue Capital ma alcuni club frenano: ecco perché l'accordo è a rischio

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Si complica la trattativa tra la Lega Serie A e Avenue Capital per la cessione di una quota del business dei diritti televisivi internazionali. Secondo quanto riportato da Reuters, alcuni club del massimo campionato italiano avrebbero espresso perplessità sull'operazione, rallentando il percorso verso un possibile accordo.

Il fondo statunitense, con sede a New York, ha messo sul tavolo 320 milioni di euro per acquisire circa il 10% della nuova società destinata a gestire i diritti internazionali della Serie A, comprese le attività legate alle trasmissioni televisive, alle sponsorizzazioni e al settore delle scommesse. Una proposta che attribuisce alla società una valutazione complessiva di 3,2 miliardi di euro.

Non tutti i club, però, sono convinti di concedere ad Avenue Capital l'esclusiva nelle trattative. Secondo tre fonti interpellate da Reuters, alcune società ritengono che la Lega debba mantenere la possibilità di esaminare eventuali offerte alternative mentre approfondisce la proposta del fondo statunitense.

Al momento Avenue Capital rimane l'unico soggetto attivamente impegnato nel negoziato, pur senza aver ottenuto formalmente l'esclusiva. Le fonti non hanno specificato quali siano i club contrari né il loro numero, ma le resistenze emerse sarebbero sufficienti a rallentare l'operazione.

Per arrivare all'approvazione definitiva sarà necessario ottenere il voto favorevole di almeno 14 delle 20 società di Serie A.

Non sarebbe la prima volta che il calcio italiano tenta di aprire agli investitori esterni. Nel 2021 era stata valutata la cessione del 10% del business dei diritti televisivi nazionali a un consorzio guidato da CVC, ma il progetto era naufragato per la mancanza del consenso necessario tra le società.

Anche questa volta, dunque, la posizione dei club sarà determinante. L'offerta di Avenue Capital rimane sul tavolo, ma l'intesa è tutt'altro che raggiunta.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Testamentary Trust - Making sure money goes to kids, not a new spouse

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I did not see poster mention it was a QTIP trust. Merely a “grantor” trust, which could be a revocable living trust.
You're right, he said "grantor trust"... but that only tells us how it was structured for income taxes while Dad was alive. What I'm wondering is, what the terms were for when his father died? Indeed, it might be a simple survivor's trust (step-mother controls everything), and if so, then his Option 2 (Dad was incompetent) is true. But I think he should check, not assume. For example, why would they be talking about annuities that "didn't get moved to the trust by accident"-- meaning, I assume, that the beneficiary designation wasn't changed from his step-mother to the trust-- if the trust is a survivor trust? The lawyer wouldn't have told you to do that, if it was all going to be controlled by his step-mother anyway?

All estates attorneys have had testators insist up and down that everybody is on the same page, they have a tight, loving family, no worries at all about that stuff... and then seen things go off like a bomb even before the funeral is over. The attorney's job is to protect their clients from things like this. And from potential creditors. And from tax law changes lowering the estate tax threshold, or if the client later moves to a state with a lower limit (... or they get a windfall, and now it's an issue). And from beneficiaries who develop drug problems, marry/procreate with dirtbags, get sued, develop dementia, etc. Nobody likes to think this is going to happen to their family, but it does happen to families, and some text in the documents up front will protect the client's interests if it does, and does no harm to anyone if it doesn't. This is why you pay a lawyer to set things up for you.

Given that the clients' desires were, his half of the estate goes to his three biological kids, her half of the estate goes to her two biological kids, I would expect the lawyer to start from an A/B trust at least, and given that CA is known as a litigious state, possibly the stronger protections from a QTIP. There would also have to be some language about successor trustees, and a natural choice would be replacing Dad with one of his biological kids, Mom with one of hers. Maybe they asked the lawyer to do something else, maybe not. So... he should take a good look at the documents, and then he'll know. (And also, BTW, make sure that the appropriate paperwork is filed with the IRS, there's a 9 month limit I believe...)

Iraq's oil output climbed to its highest level in seven years, the IEA said

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Gulf crude exports reached 18.4 million barrels a day in October, still 26% below pre-war levels, the IEA said

Iraq pumped 4.6 million barrels of crude a day in October, an increase of 540,000 barrels a day from the previous month, the International Energy Agency said in its monthly oil market report on Friday.

That was the highest level since September 2019, according to Bloomberg.

Iraq's output ran 170,000 barrels a day above its implied target of 4.43 million, the IEA said. The agency put the country's sustainable capacity at 4.87 million barrels a day.

Gulf producers excluding Oman exported 18.4 million barrels a day in October, which is 26% below pre-war levels, the IEA said. Those exports rose by 950,000 barrels a day from the prior month, according to Bloomberg. Producers in the region are sending some cargoes on low-profile tanker runs across the Strait of Hormuz and are shipping other crude by alternative export routes that never touch the strait.

Global oil supply rose by 2.3 million barrels a day to 105.4 million in October, the IEA said. That followed a 3 million barrel-a-day increase in September. The agency said the gains came from high Gulf exports despite continuing attacks on energy infrastructure and tankers.

Saudi Arabia produced 9.99 million barrels a day in October, up from 8.93 million in September, the IEA said. Kuwait produced 2.03 million barrels a day, up from 1.58 million.

The IEA's estimates differ from figures that Gulf producers submitted to OPEC. Iraq told OPEC it produced 4.888 million barrels a day in October, above the IEA's 4.6 million. Saudi Arabia told OPEC it pumped 10.563 million barrels a day, compared with the IEA's 9.99 million.

The recovery in Gulf output follows disruption from the U.S.-Israeli war on Iran, which began in late February.

Canada is threatening to sue Stellantis over Brampton plant sale

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Industry Minister Mélanie Joly said Stellantis has 30 days to address a notice of default tied to a federal funding agreement

Canada warned it may take Stellantis $STLA to court over the automaker's plan to sell its shuttered factory in Brampton, Ontario, giving it 30 days to resolve Ottawa's objections. Industry Minister Mélanie Joly told a Toronto news conference on Thursday that Ottawa had formally notified Stellantis it is in default.

"We sent a notice of default and, basically, they have 30 days — or else, of course, we will sue them," Joly told reporters, according to CNBC.

Joly said Stellantis broke the contractual promises it made to Ottawa and to Brampton's workforce when it revealed it had struck a deal to sell the facility to Roshel, which makes armored vehicles. She said the company is "on the hook" for several hundred million dollars.

Stellantis, the parent of Jeep, Dodge, Fiat, Chrysler and Peugeot, announced on Monday a deal to sell the plant to Roshel, though it did not disclose the price. The company said it recognizes what it owes the facility's employees and is ready to negotiate a settlement for union-represented workers in talks with Unifor, Canada's largest private sector union.

Stellantis Canada CEO Trevor Longley said on Thursday that the automaker will give back C$24 million in subsidies it received from Ottawa, according to CNBC. That figure falls short of what the government estimates Stellantis owes.

"We recognize the disappointment and uncertainty surrounding the future of the facility. While our decision to sell the plant represents a change from the original plans for Brampton, the agreement we have advanced with a Canadian manufacturer provides a path to return the facility to productive industrial use," Longley said, according to CNBC.

A Stellantis spokesperson was not available for comment on Friday morning, according to CNBC.

Prime Minister Mark Carney has called the situation at the plant “unacceptable” and a “betrayal” of its workers, according to CNBC.

Stellantis had dropped its plan to build the Jeep Compass at Brampton, moving future output to an Illinois factory as U.S. auto tariffs and other pressures in the market weighed on the company, according to CNBC. The U.S. and Canada are in a trade dispute, and President Donald Trump has urged carmakers to shift their vehicle manufacturing to the United States.

Nearly half of U.S. weight-loss drug users are changing how they take them, CDC finds

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Skipping doses was the most common change at 28%, and 38% of users who made changes cited cost, according to the CDC

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention released a report on Thursday showing that 44% of U.S. adults taking GLP-1 medications such as Wegovy and Zepbound changed their use of the drugs from the way they were prescribed.

Skipping doses topped the list of changes, with 28% of users saying they did it. Among users who made changes, cost was the reason for 38% and availability for 36%.

The data come from the CDC’s Rapid Surveys System, which collected responses from 8,118 U.S. adults in July and August 2025. Overall, an estimated 9.8% of adults said they had used a GLP-1 drug at some point over the past year. The 976 users in the sample received follow-up questions about their use.

Younger adults reported the most changes. Modification rates were 54% among users ages 18 to 39 and 30% among users 65 and older. Delayed prescription fills were reported by 37% of the younger group and 6% of the older group, and 58% of younger users who made changes cited cost, compared with 20% of older users.

Compounded formulations were used by 22.6% of GLP-1 users, and another 8.8% could not say if theirs was one. The Food and Drug Administration does not vet compounded GLP-1 medications for how safe, effective or well made they are before they go to market. Modification rates were 62% among compounded-drug users and 36% among other users.

About 12% of users reported reducing or stopping their medication because of side effects or symptoms. The CDC said more evidence on the safety and effectiveness of these real-world practices could help guide clinical guidelines.

The report noted limitations. The two survey panels had cumulative response rates of 13.3% and 3.8%, and the findings rely on self-reported answers.

The survey was conducted before Eli Lilly $LLY and Novo Nordisk intensified their push to make the drugs easier to get and to take on lower-priced compounded versions, according to Bloomberg. The federal government has also taken steps to broaden access, with Medicare patients among those covered.

Cost has been a barrier for some time. Without insurance or discounts, FDA-approved weight-loss drugs carry a sticker price that can top $1,000 a month, which helped drive demand for compounded versions during shortages of approved products.

The Medicare GLP-1 Bridge program, which began July 1, offers eligible beneficiaries weight-loss drugs for $50 a month.

The Midterms Are Over. History Says This Is What the Stock Market Does Next.

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Washington is divided again, and history has some encouraging data for investors.

Democrats won control of both the House and the Senate in the Nov. 3 midterm elections. That means President Trump will spend his final two years in office working with a Congress controlled by the other party. If you're wondering what this means for your portfolio, history offers some useful perspective.

Here's a statistic that sounds extremely reassuring. The S&P 500 has been higher 12 months after each of the 19 midterm elections since 1950, with an average gain of about 15%.

Even the weakest stretch, which included the 1987 crash, ended with a small gain. But the stock market has never been lower a year after a midterm election.

Five presidents since 1950 have served two full terms, and their final two years tell a mixed story. Here's how the S&P 500 did, including dividends:

One key takeaway is that the year after the midterm was positive all five times. The final year was more mixed, with two big declines. But it's worth noting why. The 2000 drop came as the dot-com bubble burst, and the 2008 drop was during the financial crisis. Neither had much to do with who controlled Congress.

Three of those presidents, Eisenhower, Reagan, and Bush, faced a Congress controlled by the other party in their final two years, just as Trump will. The results ranged from excellent to the 2008 crash. Over a longer period, RBC data shows that years with a Republican president and a Democratic Congress have averaged about a 5% annual gain, the lowest of any combination. Of course, that average is based on just a handful of periods, so I wouldn't read too much into it.

In short, history suggests that the year after a midterm tends to be good for stocks, and that a president's final stretch depends far more on the economy than on politics. Earnings, interest rates, and economic growth will drive the market over the next two years. My plan is the same as always. I'll stay invested and keep buying great businesses, no matter who controls Washington.

Once Upon a Farm is cutting its outlook after a toddler snack bar recall

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The toddler snack maker lowered its full-year net sales forecast to $290 million-$300 million from $327 million-$335 million

Once Upon a Farm lowered its full-year 2026 outlook on Thursday to reflect the voluntary recall of its Tractor Wheels product lines. The company expects net sales of $290 million to $300 million, down from the $327 million to $335 million it had forecast.

The company expects an adjusted EBITDA loss of $4.5 million to $7 million, compared with prior guidance of adjusted EBITDA of $3 million to $4.5 million. Adjusted EBITDA is earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization, with additional adjustments. The company said the recall's effect on the fourth quarter drove the update.

For the third quarter, net sales rose 34.9% to $89.1 million from $66.0 million a year earlier. Net loss narrowed to $3.8 million from $11.3 million. Adjusted EBITDA was a loss of $1.0 million, compared with a profit of $0.9 million a year earlier.

Gross margin fell to 36.1% from 42.1%. The company cited trade investment for a national club program, product mix and cooler placement costs. As of Sept. 30, it held $106.4 million in cash and cash equivalents and no debt.

"We are committed to resolving the Tractor Wheels situation correctly, continuing to earn the trust of families and retailers, and executing against the significant growth opportunity ahead of us," CEO and co-founder John Foraker said in a statement.

Foraker told Bloomberg the company anticipates as much as $30 million in recall expenses, spanning written-off inventory, legal fees and product returns. For 2027, Foraker expects the company to reach breakeven or better on an adjusted EBITDA basis, with net sales no lower than $330 million, according to Bloomberg.

The company plans to change the bars' recipe and packaging before bringing them back to store shelves, Foraker told Bloomberg. It has brought in outside specialists in food safety to review the product, according to Bloomberg.

Once Upon a Farm announced the voluntary recall of its Tractor Wheels soft-baked bars on Oct. 30, citing three consumer complaints about a choking hazard. The bars are labeled for toddlers ages 1.5 to 2.5, and the recall covers all lots across three varieties.

Tractor Wheels accounted for about 18% of the company's revenue this year as of the earlier coverage. Once Upon a Farm stock has lost over 40% of its value since it began trading in February.

$1,000 in Main Street Capital's 2007 IPO Is Worth About $7,300 Counting Dividends. It Has Never Cut Its Monthly Payout.

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Main Street Capital is one of the best, most consistent dividend stocks out there.

Investors looking for a good dividend-income stock may not find too many better than Main Street Capital (MAIN -0.78%).

The business development company (BDC) has raised its dividend for 19 straight years, every year since it went public in 2007 -- and has never once reduced its dividend.

And it has already raised its dividend for the first three months of 2027, putting it on track for 20 straight years of dividend raises.

Main Street Capital is also one of the relatively few dividend stocks that pay a monthly dividend, so the income stream comes more frequently than most.

BDCs, like real estate investment trusts (REITs), are required by law to pay out 90% of their taxable income in dividends in exchange for getting certain tax advantages. So they are literally built to generate dividend income.

Main Street Capital went public in 2007 as a BDC that focuses on providing debt and equity capital solutions for mostly smaller companies that can't get funding from traditional commercial banks or private equity partners.

But what makes it different, and superior to most, is that its portfolio is internally managed, as opposed to being managed by a third party, so it has lower fees because it doesn't pay fees to a third party. But it also takes an ownership stake in its companies, in addition to its loans, so it is more invested in companies' success and can directly benefit from their growth.

That has helped it steadily feed its dividend for 19 years. It currently pays out a monthly dividend of $0.265 through the end of 2026, up from $0.255 in the same period a year ago. Main Street Capital currently pays out a high yield of about 7.68%.

On Nov. 3, Main Street approved a $0.27 monthly dividend for the first three months of 2027 -- marking a 3.8% increase over the first quarter of 2026. This would be the 20th straight year of dividend increases for Main Street Capital. It also authorized an additional $0.30 per share supplemental dividend for December 2026 because of excess taxable income.

Main Street Capital went public in October 2007 at roughly $15 per share. If you invested $1,000 in the stock at its IPO, that would have bought you roughly 67 shares. At its current share price of around $57 per share, that $1,000 would be worth about $3,800 today.

But then how much dividend income would you have accumulated?

Calculating all dividends declared to date, including the first-quarter 2027 dividends and the supplemental December 2026 dividend, Main Street will have paid $52.315 per share in cash dividends since its October 2007 IPO. If you multiply $52.315 times the 67 shares, you would have pocketed about $3,500 in dividend income, alongside the approximately 7.2% average annualized return you made on Main Street's stock since its IPO.

So, if you combined the dividend income with the capital appreciation, that $1,000 investment in 2007 would be worth about $7,300 today.

Inside the $3.25 Million Pasadena Glass Pavilion That’s Still Studied in Architecture Classes Today

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A three-bedroom midcentury modern home designed by Case Study architect Craig Ellwood has just come on the market in Pasadena, CA, for $3.25 million. It features a notable cantilevered glass pavilion.

Built in 1965, the Kubly House was originally constructed for less than $45,000. The 2,227-square-foot home has since been thoughtfully updated.

“What makes this house special is its architecture and design, and how true it has remained to the original,” says Chris Suarez, who is co-listing the property with Kimberly Benz of Suarez + Benz at Compass. “Nothing was significantly altered. Everything done was intended to restore the house to its original condition. The footprint, the floors, the glass—everything has remained true to the original design.”

Located in the Linda Vista neighborhood, the home has an iconic redwood, cantilevered glass pavilion that opens to a single-level floor plan. The private half-acre lot includes a pool and spa surrounded by lush landscaping with eucalyptus and oak trees.

With exposed post-and-beam construction, clean horizontal lines, and expansive walls of glass, the home was designed to give the illusion of floating above its natural surroundings. The deck was recently restored using specially milled wood.

“It was sourced from northern Washington and brought down here,” Suarez notes. “Everything was done painstakingly to avoid altering the house’s integrity, architecture, and design. That being said, significant work has been done, including updates to the roof, infrastructure, air conditioning, and electrical systems. All the major systems have been updated, but you wouldn’t necessarily know it because the work was done so carefully.”

Originally commissioned by ArtCenter College of Design alumni and educators Don and Sally Kubly, the home represents their understanding of design and the culture of the college community. It is still studied in classes there.

“Don was ArtCenter’s first president in Pasadena,” Suarez explains. “There’s also a connection through Craig Ellwood, who designed ArtCenter’s main campus building. The main campus is an incredible steel-and-glass building designed by Ellwood. Don played a significant role in moving the school from Los Angeles to Pasadena, where he served as president for many years. He’s very well known within that community, and this house is significant to the college’s history and culture.”

Based on available architectural records, the Kubly House is said to be Ellwood’s only documented residential project in Pasadena.

After remaining in the Kubly family for nearly six decades, the home changed hands for $3.6 million in 2022. Since then, the sellers have undertaken a preservation-minded restoration with Carl Bronson of Bronstruction, updating major systems to contemporary standards.

“A house that helped usher in much midcentury modernism was the Glass House, designed by Philip Johnson around 1948 or 1949,” Benz notes. “That design was definitely something Ellwood loved. You can clearly see that house’s influence here, which is important because it reveals some of the influences behind Ellwood’s work.”

Benz adds that the home’s design was also influenced by Ellwood’s peers, including midcentury designer George Nelson.

“A furniture plan developed for the house clearly referenced Mies van der Rohe and George Nelson, both important figures in midcentury modern design,” she says. “Even the deck and the steps leading to the house resemble a George Nelson bench. There’s actually a bench built into the concrete wall near the pool that resembles a George Nelson design. I don’t know whether Nelson designed or made it, but the influence is certainly there.”

The house is special because many of the original details have remained intact, she adds.

“You can see the influence of the people Ellwood admired throughout the house,” Benz says. “That significance is what makes this house important when you look at the midcentury modernist movement. All these examples are visible in the house and remain there.”

Some of the built-in furniture has a distinctive style, too.

“There’s a stunning floating credenza that looks like a George Nelson piece,” she notes. “We think it might be one of his designs. It’s in the living room. There’s also a built-in console that might be a George Nelson design as well. So, some of the furniture is actually built into the architecture.”

While some pieces are originals and others are reproductions, Benz says, “the most important thing was following the original plan. The history of this house is everything.”

When staging the home, she says they were “fortunate to discover an original interior design study among the blueprints, plans, and documents that came with the house. We were able to look at that study, and it became a guide and a road map for staging the property. Sourcing the furniture was a challenge because we had to find the specific pieces.”

The city of Pasadena has also identified the property as eligible for individual listing on the National Register of Historic Places.

13 Fab Old Photos of 2026 Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Inductee Sade Adu

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Think Sade, and a few things likely pop to mind. There’s the signature beauty look: the dark eyeliner, shapely brows, red lip, and long, slicked-back plait. There are the clothes: good earrings—often big gold hoops—and stage outfits that were sexy, yes, but also slyly demure; high-waisted trousers, semi-sheer turtlenecks, cropped jackets, qipao-style dresses. You may nerdily make the distinction between Sade the woman—Helen Folasade Adu, OBE, born on January 16, 1959, in Ibadan, Nigeria—and Sade the Grammy-winning band, formed in 1982 and made up of Adu, Paul Denman, Andrew Hale, and Stuart Matthewman.

Now there’s one more thing to add to that list: the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame. This weekend, the band will celebrate their induction—along with acts including Phil Collins, Billy Idol, Oasis, and the late Luther Vandross—in a ceremony at Los Angeles’s Peacock Theater. Now, will any of Sade’s members actually be present for that ceremony? No, according to a statement released on November 10. Even so, they called the recognition “both a gift and a timely reminder to give thanks, not only to those who voted for us but also every single person who lets our music into their lives.”

And what music that is: from “Smooth Operator” to “Kiss of Life,” “Is It a Crime” to “No Ordinary Love,” “The Sweetest Taboo” to “I Couldn’t Love You More,” Sade’s soulful, sui generis sound remains a benchmark for timeless cool.

Here, in celebration of her group’s latest honor, we’ve rounded up 13 striking old photographs of the divine Sade Adu, a generational talent and forever style icon.

Sporting spotty shirting, high-waisted trousers, and a wide belt during a 1984 set in Rotterdam.

This is no ordinary love–and those are no ordinary neutrals. A graduate of London’s Saint Martin’s School of Art, Sade shows off her style bonafides at the Hammersmith Odeon in 1984.

Cooler than cool in sunglasses and a simple white button-down.

Performing at the Live Aid charity concert at Wembley Stadium in 1985.

Gold jewelry, jaunty bolero jacket, can’t lose.

In textured whites—and that classic red lip—at the Ambassador East Hotel in Chicago, 1985.

Making her Saturday Night Live debut on December 14, 1985, with Tom Hanks as host. The band performed “Is It a Crime” and “The Sweetest Taboo,” both from their second studio album, Promise.

In 2010, recalling the release of Sade’s debut album, Diamond Life, in 1984, The New Yorker’s Sasha Frere-Jones poetically described Adu’s voice on as “a grainy contralto full of air that betrays a slight ache but no agony, and values even imperfect dignity over a show of pain.”

Center-stage at at Northrop Auditorium in Minneapolis, 1993.

With the English singer-songwriter Leo Sayer at a charity ball in London, 2001.

Having a moment at the United Center in Chicago, 2001.

Red all over, in concert at PNC Bank Arts Center in Holmdel, New Jersey, 2001.

Trump says he wants to ‘get Cuba done’ before leaving office, would consider military force

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

U.S. President Donald Trump has said he wants to "get Cuba done" before leaving office in 2029, adding that he would "certainly consider" military intervention against the communist-run Caribbean island.

In an interview with conservative radio host Hugh Hewitt on Thursday, Trump said his administration was monitoring the situation in Havana "very closely" and described Cuba as a "failed" state.

"I want to get Cuba done before I leave," Trump said. His comments marked the first time he has put a timeline on possible military intervention to topple Cuban authorities, according to Politico.

Trump said Secretary of State Marco Rubio, whose parents are from Cuba, "would love to do it" and said he would "certainly consider" a short military operation.

It comes as the U.S. continues to enforce a comprehensive economic embargo against Cuba, a campaign which has contributed to a deepening energy and economic crisis on the island. Cuba has suffered repeated nationwide blackouts in recent months, as well as fuel and food shortages.

The Cuban Embassy in Washington, D.C., estimated on Thursday that the cost of the U.S. blockade to Cuba between March 2025 and February 2026 amounted to $8 billion.

Earlier this month, the Treasury Department imposed further sanctions on Cuban oil tankers and shipping companies and threatened to target any international entity or person doing business within its marine or maritime industries as part of a push "to end the Cuban regime's malign activities, both in Cuba and across our hemisphere."

In response, Cuba's Embassy in Washington, D.C., said these steps "deepen an energy siege that already leaves hospitals without reliable power and families facing blackouts. The blockade is not targeted at a government. It is collective punishment that hits ordinary people every day."

Trump has previously talked up the prospect of a "friendly takeover" of Havana and said the White House could turn its sights on Cuba after the Iran war. The U.S. president has also said he could do anything he wanted with the country, adding that he thinks he will have the "honor" of "taking Cuba."

In May, Cuban Energy Minister Vicente de la O Levy said the country of roughly 10 million had run out of fuel oil and diesel, describing the country's predicament as "extremely tense."

Analysts have typically been skeptical about the prospect of a U.S. military campaign against Cuba, suggesting that further economic pressure against the island's authorities appears more likely.

Jensen Huang didn’t show confidence in his successor, report says

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Former NVIDIA CEO Jensen Huang described Collette Kress, the company’s CFO who has now replaced him as interim CEO, as “a deer in the headlights” during a presentation earlier in the summer, the Wall Street Journal reported, citing an anonymous source.

Huang made the comment during a meeting with Nvidia’s finance team about the company’s plans to provide guarantees worth billions of dollars to data center operators that couldn’t afford to buy its chips upfront, according to the report.

He reportedly also said Kress was a person who couldn’t help him steer strategy or decision-making.

Kress has been with Nvidia for over 13 years now, after serving as SVP and CFO of Cisco’s Business Technology Operations Finance. The report said she has questioned Nvidia’s program-focused, credit-based deals involving Huang’s son-in-law Nico Caprez, and wanted to know how to decide which deals are beneficial, how to value guarantees, and determine returns.

Earlier in the year, she had scaled back on Nvidia’s commitment for OpenAI’s ambitious Ohio data center project. The company was in talks to provide $250 billion in credit to OpenAI, but ended up committing $105 billion.

Huang resigned Thursday after reports of two extramarital affairs, including one with the company’s VP for healthcare, Kimberly Powell. The company is also investigating a possible Chinese espionage plot.

After his resignation, Reuters reported that he was having a third affair with another employee. Nvidia is currently searching for a permanent CEO to replace Huang, who had the top job for over 33 years.

Coinbase to Delist 12 Crypto Trading Pairs, ADA-ETH and XLM-BTC Among Them

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Major crypto exchange Coinbase is set to suspend trading in 12 crypto trading pairs as part of an effort to improve overall market health and consolidate liquidity on the platform. This decision affects two trading pairs involving Cardano (ADA) and Stellar (XLM): ADA/ETH and XLM/BTC.

Coinbase said it regularly monitors the markets on its exchange and, in line with this, trading will be suspended on the named markets on November 25, 2026. In an effort to improve overall market health and consolidate liquidity, Coinbase will be removing a number of non-USD trading pairs for supported assets.

According to an announcement, Coinbase will suspend trading on the following markets on November 25, 2026, at or around 12 PM ET: APE-USDT, ATOM-USDT, ALGO-BTC, CRV-BTC, ETC-BTC, ICP-BTC, XLM-BTC, APE-EUR, ADA-ETH, 1INCH-GBP, CHZ-GBP, SNX-GBP.

However, Coinbase Advanced Trade users in eligible regions can still trade these assets in the USD order books.

In light of this, APE-USDT, ATOM-USDT, ALGO-BTC, CRV-BTC, ETC-BTC, ICP-BTC, XLM-BTC, APE-EUR, ADA-ETH, 1INCH-GBP, CHZ-GBP have been moved to limit-only mode on Coinbase Exchange and Coinbase Advanced.

In recent delistings, Coinbase has disabled trading for Balancer (BAL) and Tensor (TNSR), but users will continue to have the ability to withdraw their funds at any time.

The suspension of trading for Stargate Finance (STG) and Vulcan Forged (PYR) has also been postponed to November 20, 2026, at or around 2 PM ET. Based on recent reviews, Coinbase will suspend trading for Ampleforth Governance Token (FORTH) on November 23, 2026, at or around 2 PM ET. Trading for Neon EVM (NEON) and Syndicate (SYND) will be suspended on November 16, 2026, at or around 2 PM ET.

Spot trading for Pearl (PEARL) will go live on November 13, 2026. The opening of the PEARL-USD trading pair will begin at or after 9 AM PT, if liquidity conditions are met, in regions where trading is supported.

Perpetual futures trading for GRAM, GRASS, QNT and ZRO will go live on November 13, 2026, at or after 12:00 PM UTC.

Cemig and Huawei Jointly Deploy 450 MHz Private Wireless Network

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

JOHANNESBURG, Nov. 13, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- As distribution network services grew increasingly complex, Cemig's distribution devices relied primarily on leased public networks for data backhaul. However, this model presented significant risks.

To address these challenges, Cemig conducted multiple rounds of technical evaluation and global use case research, ultimately selecting Huawei as its strategic partner to jointly deploy the first commercial 450 MHz private wireless network for the power sector in Latin America.

After field testing, the customer gained firsthand experience of the benefits delivered by the private wireless network, strengthening their confidence in distribution grid modernization and raising high expectations for the future energy transition: "Huawei submitted the best proposal during the bidding process, offering a solution that not only meets our current distribution grid modernization requirements but also scales for future renewable energy integration. Huawei has extensive experience in the energy industry. With Huawei's private network solution, Cemig will secure a high-quality private communications network. For Cemig, this partnership goes far beyond technology; it is crucial to our future business growth."

1. Improved power supply reliabilityThe private wireless network lifted distribution terminal connectivity to 99% and communication reliability to 99.99%, significantly improving Cemig's power distribution service levels.

2. Mitigated business risksThe private wireless network can significantly improve line losses, SAIDI, and SAIFI, thereby helping Cemig avoid regulatory penalties.

3. Assured investment returnsThe private wireless network greatly enhances power services, ensuring Cemig meets ANEEL's regulatory requirements and secures favorable investment returns.

4. Expanded core production applicationsAs the foundational communications backbone for power production operations, the private wireless network delivers reliable last-mile connectivity for today's distribution automation. Furthermore, it supports future core applications, such as massive smart meter integration, rapid distributed PV adoption, and complex microgrid management, driving the transition from traditional load-based power generation to dynamic interaction across source, grid, load, and storage.

New York Times Blames Trump For Air Safety Risks—Gets Blasted By The Independent NTSB

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The National Transportation Safety Board says the New York Times distorted its comments to attack President Trump. The paper’s aviation safety story has bigger problems than just politics. It gets a major crash wrong and credits a pilot hours rule for a safety record that began years before the rule took effect. James Fallows’s “Is Air Travel Getting Less Safe?” describes real issues of overworked air traffic controllers, military aircraft operating around passenger flights, and safety recommendations that haven’t been adequately addressed. Then it tries to turn them into a story about Trump dismantling the institutions that keep us safe, even though the issues he cites have been going on far longer than the current administration. I’ve written predominantly critically about Trump since he first ran for president 11 years ago. I rejected his effort to blame diversity hiring for the American Airlines 5342 tragedy before investigators had established what happened. That doesn’t make every argument against him a good one. The Times introduces Fallows as an instrument-rated private pilot with more than 2,000 hours. That’s true but while he’s written extensively about aviation it’s an odd way to frame him as author of this story. He’s also Jimmy Carter’s former chief speechwriter, a longtime Atlantic writer and editor, and a familiar voice on NPR. His career has been about politics and public policy. His own introduction to the article frames the piece as meant to connect aviation under Trump with public health, international aid, diplomacy, higher education and the rule of law. Aviation is the vehicle for a larger political point. The piece isn’t really about aviation, it’s using aviation. The Independent NTSB Attacks The Piece The NTSB says the article: Distorts the agency’s responses with political statements intended to attack the current administration. Mischaracterizes the NTSB’s role in the 1,500 hour pilot requirement. Incorrectly implies negligence by the Black Hawk pilots involved in the Washington National Airport collision. The statement also thanks the administration for supporting the agency and providing resources. That last part is political messaging too. (Gratitude for a budget doesn’t answer address aviation safety, but is probably necessary as a defensive measure.) “We are disappointed that this article sensationalizes and politicizes tragedies.” The NTSB’s response to the New York Times Magazine. — NTSB Newsroom (@NTSB_Newsroom) November 12, 2026 Fallows interviewed NTSB Chair Jennifer Homendy. The agency argues she’s being mischaracterized, but they don’t actually say how. In the article she says, “Recommendations alone don’t save lives. Action does.” Her own July statement about the Washington crash warned that delays implementing the board’s recommendations represented a continuing, unacceptable risk. The Article Gets The Colgan Air Crash Wrong Fallows spends considerable space describing the 2009 Colgan Air crash near Buffalo – as the last U.S. airline crash with loss of life before American 5342. In fact, PenAir flight 3296 overran the runway at Unalaska in October 2019, killing a passenger. His account has the Colgan Air plane accumulating ice until it becomes a “frigid brick.” He writes that by the time the captain recognized the problem, it “might already have become unflyable.” But that isn’t what the investigation found. The FAA’s accident analysis says icing wasn’t a cause. When the stall warning activated, the airplane wasn’t yet stalled. The captain pulled back on the controls and put it into a stall. The crew had failed to monitor their airspeed and mishandled the approach. And both Colgan pilots already had more than 1,500 hours: The captain had 3,379 hours. The captain had a history of training failures despite the hours. The first officer had 2,244 hours, including 1,470 hours as a light airplane flight instructor and 774 hours in turbine aircraft. The 1,500 Hour Rule For Co-Pilots Isn’t The Reason For The Safety Improvements After Colgan, Congress and regulators made several changes. Pilot rest rules improved. Training changed. Pilot qualification requirements changed. Sharing pilot records improved. Evidence that safety has performed well after a package of changes doesn’t establish that (1) every element of those changes (2) caused the improvements. And there’s strong reason to believe the the 1,500 hour rule has the opposite effect. It makes becoming a pilot take far longer and become far more costly, which keeps talented people from becoming pilots. Those with a higher opportunity cost, or lacking access to resources needed, get excluded from the pool of potential pilots. You’re then drawing from a smaller group. The NTSB didn’t recommend the 1,500 hour number. In 2010 Senate testimony, then-chair Deborah Hersman explained that the board had investigated accidents involving both high-time and low-time pilots. It found a relationship between failed practical tests and subsequent accidents, but there wasn’t evidence that number of hours actually mattered. The higher minimum took effect in 2013. Fallows’s safety streak begins in February 2009. The first four and a half years of that streak can’t be credited to a requirement that wasn’t yet in effect. Making pilots chase hours can allow relevant skills to deteriorate and bad habits to develop before an airline has to train those habits out of them. Repeated flights in clear weather, making takeoffs and landings at the same familiar airports, aren’t equivalent to structured training for instrument flying in a jet with another pilot, airline procedures, and complex air traffic control instructions. Hours in a hot air balloon count. The hot air balloon can be tethered. European co-pilots don’t face the same restriction, but their safety record is equivalent. Sub-1,500 hour European pilots fly in the United States every day without concern (and American pilots fly in Europe without elevated risk). The argument for the rule is that experience can build judgment, and that the higher threshold was part of a successful improvement in regional airline standards. But that’s an argument for useful experience and rigorous standards. That’s not what we got. An hour spent building time isn’t as valuable as an hour spent in a supervised airline training program. And there’s zero evidence that 1,500 is the right metric. It was chosen because it’s expensive and time-consuming, restricts entry into the pilot profession and drives up wages, and was supported by the major pilots union. The Black Hawk Crew Bears Responsibility The NTSB objected to Fallows implying negligence by the helicopter pilots. But its own probable cause finding identifies the helicopter crew’s failure to maintain effective visual separation as a cause of the collision. The crew accepted responsibility for avoiding the airliner and failed to do so. The existence of systemic failures don’t eliminate pilot error. In Homendy’s testimony explaining the findings, the board concluded that the helicopter’s altimeter probably understated its altitude by about 100 feet. The crew likely believed it was below the route’s ceiling. Fallows also describes the crew flying through civilian traffic without monitoring its traffic avoidance systems. The helicopter had no integrated cockpit traffic display. The pilots had tablets capable of showing traffic, but looking down at them while flying low at night with night vision goggles was problematic. Their audio alerts weren’t integrated into the pilots’ helmets. There were failures of FAA route design, and the FAA failed to act in the face of repeated warnings. But it’s genuinely weird to pin the blame for that on the first nine days of the Trump administration before the crash. I’ve criticized efforts to preserve military exceptions around Washington National. Civilian aviation shouldn’t have to accommodate unnecessary military risks. The Pentagon needs to change, and the current administration is responsible for making that happen. But it didn’t invent the culture that allowed these risks to exist in the first place. The Article’s Own Incident Data Doesn’t Support A Partisan Cause Fallows combines airline accidents, rocket debris, military encounters, airshow incidents and presidential flyovers which generally have different causes. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative: 2023: 22 incidents. 2024: 7 incidents. 2025: 14 incidents. 2026 through October: 9 incidents. The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.) Air Traffic Control Was Broken Before Trump Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider. (HT: Paddle Your Own Kanoo) Topics on this pageAviation safetyDonald TrumpJames FallowsNational Transportation Safety BoardThe New York TimesAir traffic controlAmerican AirlinesBuffaloCanadaColgan AirColgan Air Flight 3407Federal Aviation AdministrationJimmy CarterJoe BidenNational Public RadioRonald Reagan Washington National AirportSean DuffyThe TimesUnited States CongressUnited States Senate+15 more More From View from the Wing

James Fallows’s “Is Air Travel Getting Less Safe?” describes real issues of overworked air traffic controllers, military aircraft operating around passenger flights, and safety recommendations that haven’t been adequately addressed. Then it tries to turn them into a story about Trump dismantling the institutions that keep us safe, even though the issues he cites have been going on far longer than the current administration.

I’ve written predominantly critically about Trump since he first ran for president 11 years ago. I rejected his effort to blame diversity hiring for the American Airlines 5342 tragedy before investigators had established what happened. That doesn’t make every argument against him a good one.

The Times introduces Fallows as an instrument-rated private pilot with more than 2,000 hours. That’s true but while he’s written extensively about aviation it’s an odd way to frame him as author of this story.

He’s also Jimmy Carter’s former chief speechwriter, a longtime Atlantic writer and editor, and a familiar voice on NPR. His career has been about politics and public policy.

His own introduction to the article frames the piece as meant to connect aviation under Trump with public health, international aid, diplomacy, higher education and the rule of law. Aviation is the vehicle for a larger political point. The piece isn’t really about aviation, it’s using aviation. The Independent NTSB Attacks The Piece The NTSB says the article: Distorts the agency’s responses with political statements intended to attack the current administration. Mischaracterizes the NTSB’s role in the 1,500 hour pilot requirement. Incorrectly implies negligence by the Black Hawk pilots involved in the Washington National Airport collision. The statement also thanks the administration for supporting the agency and providing resources. That last part is political messaging too. (Gratitude for a budget doesn’t answer address aviation safety, but is probably necessary as a defensive measure.) “We are disappointed that this article sensationalizes and politicizes tragedies.” The NTSB’s response to the New York Times Magazine. — NTSB Newsroom (@NTSB_Newsroom) November 12, 2026 Fallows interviewed NTSB Chair Jennifer Homendy. The agency argues she’s being mischaracterized, but they don’t actually say how. In the article she says, “Recommendations alone don’t save lives. Action does.” Her own July statement about the Washington crash warned that delays implementing the board’s recommendations represented a continuing, unacceptable risk. The Article Gets The Colgan Air Crash Wrong Fallows spends considerable space describing the 2009 Colgan Air crash near Buffalo – as the last U.S. airline crash with loss of life before American 5342. In fact, PenAir flight 3296 overran the runway at Unalaska in October 2019, killing a passenger. His account has the Colgan Air plane accumulating ice until it becomes a “frigid brick.” He writes that by the time the captain recognized the problem, it “might already have become unflyable.” But that isn’t what the investigation found. The FAA’s accident analysis says icing wasn’t a cause. When the stall warning activated, the airplane wasn’t yet stalled. The captain pulled back on the controls and put it into a stall. The crew had failed to monitor their airspeed and mishandled the approach. And both Colgan pilots already had more than 1,500 hours: The captain had 3,379 hours. The captain had a history of training failures despite the hours. The first officer had 2,244 hours, including 1,470 hours as a light airplane flight instructor and 774 hours in turbine aircraft. The 1,500 Hour Rule For Co-Pilots Isn’t The Reason For The Safety Improvements After Colgan, Congress and regulators made several changes. Pilot rest rules improved. Training changed. Pilot qualification requirements changed. Sharing pilot records improved. Evidence that safety has performed well after a package of changes doesn’t establish that (1) every element of those changes (2) caused the improvements. And there’s strong reason to believe the the 1,500 hour rule has the opposite effect. It makes becoming a pilot take far longer and become far more costly, which keeps talented people from becoming pilots. Those with a higher opportunity cost, or lacking access to resources needed, get excluded from the pool of potential pilots. You’re then drawing from a smaller group. The NTSB didn’t recommend the 1,500 hour number. In 2010 Senate testimony, then-chair Deborah Hersman explained that the board had investigated accidents involving both high-time and low-time pilots. It found a relationship between failed practical tests and subsequent accidents, but there wasn’t evidence that number of hours actually mattered. The higher minimum took effect in 2013. Fallows’s safety streak begins in February 2009. The first four and a half years of that streak can’t be credited to a requirement that wasn’t yet in effect. Making pilots chase hours can allow relevant skills to deteriorate and bad habits to develop before an airline has to train those habits out of them. Repeated flights in clear weather, making takeoffs and landings at the same familiar airports, aren’t equivalent to structured training for instrument flying in a jet with another pilot, airline procedures, and complex air traffic control instructions. Hours in a hot air balloon count. The hot air balloon can be tethered. European co-pilots don’t face the same restriction, but their safety record is equivalent. Sub-1,500 hour European pilots fly in the United States every day without concern (and American pilots fly in Europe without elevated risk). The argument for the rule is that experience can build judgment, and that the higher threshold was part of a successful improvement in regional airline standards. But that’s an argument for useful experience and rigorous standards. That’s not what we got. An hour spent building time isn’t as valuable as an hour spent in a supervised airline training program. And there’s zero evidence that 1,500 is the right metric. It was chosen because it’s expensive and time-consuming, restricts entry into the pilot profession and drives up wages, and was supported by the major pilots union. The Black Hawk Crew Bears Responsibility The NTSB objected to Fallows implying negligence by the helicopter pilots. But its own probable cause finding identifies the helicopter crew’s failure to maintain effective visual separation as a cause of the collision. The crew accepted responsibility for avoiding the airliner and failed to do so. The existence of systemic failures don’t eliminate pilot error. In Homendy’s testimony explaining the findings, the board concluded that the helicopter’s altimeter probably understated its altitude by about 100 feet. The crew likely believed it was below the route’s ceiling. Fallows also describes the crew flying through civilian traffic without monitoring its traffic avoidance systems. The helicopter had no integrated cockpit traffic display. The pilots had tablets capable of showing traffic, but looking down at them while flying low at night with night vision goggles was problematic. Their audio alerts weren’t integrated into the pilots’ helmets. There were failures of FAA route design, and the FAA failed to act in the face of repeated warnings. But it’s genuinely weird to pin the blame for that on the first nine days of the Trump administration before the crash. I’ve criticized efforts to preserve military exceptions around Washington National. Civilian aviation shouldn’t have to accommodate unnecessary military risks. The Pentagon needs to change, and the current administration is responsible for making that happen. But it didn’t invent the culture that allowed these risks to exist in the first place. The Article’s Own Incident Data Doesn’t Support A Partisan Cause Fallows combines airline accidents, rocket debris, military encounters, airshow incidents and presidential flyovers which generally have different causes. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative: 2023: 22 incidents. 2024: 7 incidents. 2025: 14 incidents. 2026 through October: 9 incidents. The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.) Air Traffic Control Was Broken Before Trump Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider. The Pentagon needs to change, and the current administration is responsible for making that happen. But it didn’t invent the culture that allowed these risks to exist in the first place. The Article’s Own Incident Data Doesn’t Support A Partisan Cause Fallows combines airline accidents, rocket debris, military encounters, airshow incidents and presidential flyovers which generally have different causes. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative: 2023: 22 incidents. 2024: 7 incidents. 2025: 14 incidents. 2026 through October: 9 incidents. The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.) Air Traffic Control Was Broken Before Trump Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider. (HT: Paddle Your Own Kanoo) Topics on this pageAviation safetyDonald TrumpJames FallowsNational Transportation Safety BoardThe New York TimesAir traffic controlAmerican AirlinesBuffaloCanadaColgan AirColgan Air Flight 3407Federal Aviation AdministrationJimmy CarterJoe BidenNational Public RadioRonald Reagan Washington National AirportSean DuffyThe TimesUnited States CongressUnited States Senate+15 more More From View from the Wing

After Colgan, Congress and regulators made several changes. Pilot rest rules improved. Training changed. Pilot qualification requirements changed. Sharing pilot records improved. Evidence that safety has performed well after a package of changes doesn’t establish that (1) every element of those changes (2) caused the improvements.

And there’s strong reason to believe the the 1,500 hour rule has the opposite effect. It makes becoming a pilot take far longer and become far more costly, which keeps talented people from becoming pilots. Those with a higher opportunity cost, or lacking access to resources needed, get excluded from the pool of potential pilots. You’re then drawing from a smaller group.

The NTSB didn’t recommend the 1,500 hour number. In 2010 Senate testimony, then-chair Deborah Hersman explained that the board had investigated accidents involving both high-time and low-time pilots. It found a relationship between failed practical tests and subsequent accidents, but there wasn’t evidence that number of hours actually mattered.

The higher minimum took effect in 2013. Fallows’s safety streak begins in February 2009. The first four and a half years of that streak can’t be credited to a requirement that wasn’t yet in effect.

Making pilots chase hours can allow relevant skills to deteriorate and bad habits to develop before an airline has to train those habits out of them. Repeated flights in clear weather, making takeoffs and landings at the same familiar airports, aren’t equivalent to structured training for instrument flying in a jet with another pilot, airline procedures, and complex air traffic control instructions.

Hours in a hot air balloon count. The hot air balloon can be tethered.

European co-pilots don’t face the same restriction, but their safety record is equivalent. Sub-1,500 hour European pilots fly in the United States every day without concern (and American pilots fly in Europe without elevated risk). The argument for the rule is that experience can build judgment, and that the higher threshold was part of a successful improvement in regional airline standards. But that’s an argument for useful experience and rigorous standards. That’s not what we got. An hour spent building time isn’t as valuable as an hour spent in a supervised airline training program. And there’s zero evidence that 1,500 is the right metric. It was chosen because it’s expensive and time-consuming, restricts entry into the pilot profession and drives up wages, and was supported by the major pilots union. The Black Hawk Crew Bears Responsibility The NTSB objected to Fallows implying negligence by the helicopter pilots. But its own probable cause finding identifies the helicopter crew’s failure to maintain effective visual separation as a cause of the collision. The crew accepted responsibility for avoiding the airliner and failed to do so. The existence of systemic failures don’t eliminate pilot error. In Homendy’s testimony explaining the findings, the board concluded that the helicopter’s altimeter probably understated its altitude by about 100 feet. The crew likely believed it was below the route’s ceiling. Fallows also describes the crew flying through civilian traffic without monitoring its traffic avoidance systems. The helicopter had no integrated cockpit traffic display. The pilots had tablets capable of showing traffic, but looking down at them while flying low at night with night vision goggles was problematic. Their audio alerts weren’t integrated into the pilots’ helmets. There were failures of FAA route design, and the FAA failed to act in the face of repeated warnings. But it’s genuinely weird to pin the blame for that on the first nine days of the Trump administration before the crash. I’ve criticized efforts to preserve military exceptions around Washington National. Civilian aviation shouldn’t have to accommodate unnecessary military risks. The Pentagon needs to change, and the current administration is responsible for making that happen. But it didn’t invent the culture that allowed these risks to exist in the first place. The Article’s Own Incident Data Doesn’t Support A Partisan Cause Fallows combines airline accidents, rocket debris, military encounters, airshow incidents and presidential flyovers which generally have different causes. And a list that attributes incidents while a given President is in office to that President doesn’t do a good job of evaluating what decisions caused those incidents. But more simply, even the article’s own figures for the most serious runway incursions are problematic for the Fallows narrative: 2023: 22 incidents. 2024: 7 incidents. 2025: 14 incidents. 2026 through October: 9 incidents. The increase from 2024 to 2025 looks dramatic – a doubling! But it’s still far lower than under Biden in 2023! These are relatively small numbers, and they don’t show a steadily worsening trend. (And there are more aircraft operations than in 2023.) Air Traffic Control Was Broken Before Trump Fallows points to deregulation and falling fares as sources of overcrowding. More people being able to afford to fly is a good thing, and flying is still safer than driving. The failure is an FAA air traffic organization system that hasn’t kept up with demand. The Times has done excellent reporting on this subject. In August 2023, I covered its investigation into 46 close calls in one month and roughly 300 near collisions in a year. Staffing shortages, fatigue, outdated equipment and a culture that tolerated recurring warnings weren’t the result of the 2024 election. Fallows discusses 132 union-represented FAA employees fired during the DOGE purge. Their union announced their reinstatement in March 2025. Firing them was disruptive, but he leaves out the reversal. And recruiting gamers doesn’t establish that the FAA is lowering controller standards. The FAA has been growing the top of the funnel but there’s no indication that unqualified people are being advanced in training to a greater degree. I’ve criticized Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy for counting initial trainee hires as progress, that strikes me as juking the stats but hasn’t been a safety issue. And the Trump administration is doing modernization work that’s valuable and that’s been ignored for decades even if I’m skeptical that it’s close to enough because they don’t seem to be addressing the management problems at the FAA that created failed technology investment and decades-long controller shortages in the first place. I’ve been critical of pouring money into the existing organization. We need to address how air traffic control is managed, how it buys equipment, how it finances long projects and how it’s held accountable for performance. We’d be far better off separating the air traffic control operator from its regulator, with stable funding and professional management, as Canada has done with its nonprofit provider.
